Practicing, a hermeneutic of charity, a hermeneutic of submission. What is God doing in this text? What is Paul doing in the text? How can I give, if it is the word of God and God is understood by us to be all good, all loving, merciful, kind, compassionate, holy, just, how do I submit my will to his? How do I submit to what God is doing in the text? And I think that’s the great payoff of Speech Act Theory. It explains what is actually taking place. God does do things in the text and the things that he wants to do in his text, in his word, in us, his children, is wonderful.

<h2 id="lesson-6-thinking-theologically-about-meaning">Lesson 6: Thinking Theologically About Meaning</h2>

### I. Review of Where Meaning of the Text Comes From

Let’s think about what meaning is, and let’s do that from a theological perspective here. Where does meaning come from? We might say, “Well, the text…you’ve been saying that over and over again. ‘Is there a meaning in this text? What does the text say?’” Of course, the text was written by someone. The text really doesn’t convey meaning autonomously. It’s like, I mean, what is a text? It’s paper and ink. It’s inanimate. How can an inanimate object give meaning? Meaning is really a thought process. Meaning is a construction of thought. The text doesn’t construct thought; it conveys thought.

Well, then who reads the text? Maybe it’s the reader that creates meaning. And of course, I’ve already denied that – that was that reader-response idea, the “what it means to me.” And it is not that the reader discerns the meaning, but that the reader gives meaning to the text that is implied in reader-response. And I’ve already said, “No, that’s not the case or it ought not to be the case.” Is the text a window or a mirror? Does it in some way facilitate our own illumination? Does it give us access to another world? And of course, as I said before, meaning is conveyed by an author. It’s the author who is to control meaning.

And so as, again, another Kevin Vanhoozer quote, I’ve quoted him a number of times now, he says hermeneutics is inevitable. Not because the biblical texts are unclear, but because the aims and interests of the interpreter often are. God is a very good communicator. The biblical authors are very, very good writers. Not everything they say is equally clear, but most of the time, most of the time, the fault lies with us, the readers who are trying to interpret this. So where does meaning come from? It comes from the author. And as I’ve said before, the Scriptures have dual authorship. The Scriptures are all inspired by God. They are, every word of the Scriptures, the Word of God. And yet simultaneously, every word is a human word, written by a human author.

### II. Presuppositions to Understanding the Bible

So, if that’s the case and if hermeneutics is inevitable or necessary because readers and interpreters have different aims and goals, having a theological interest is not arbitrary. It’s actually necessary. We have to understand who God is in order to rightly read the Scriptures. If we’re going to do justice to the nature of the Bible itself, we have to understand it to be what it is: a collection of human speech acts, certainly, but also a unified divine speech act as well. And so, we have to know something about who God is. And here are some basic presuppositions, it seems to me that we have to have if we’re to understand the Bible correctly.

#### A. Our doctrine of Scripture is related to our doctrine of God

Our doctrine of Scripture is ultimately directly related to our doctrine of God. And here’s one very obvious presupposition we have to have: God is the Lord. We find that in Genesis 1:1 right off the bat. You might notice that we’re not given any sort of apologetic. There’s no like preamble; there’s no prologue that argues for the existence of God. We’re just told right off the bat: God is the creator of everything. And throughout redemptive history, God seeks to identify himself to people as Lord and to teach and demonstrate to them the meaning of that concept. We could say that God is the Lord is the message of the Old Testament, and then it narrows down to Jesus Christ is the Lord is the message of the New Testament.

But this Lordship in the Bible is always covenantal. Lordship is covenantal The covenants are those pacts, those agreements, those contracts that God makes. And they are not contracts between equals. God is God and we are not. In the Old Testament. God as covenant Lord, he selected a certain people from all the nations of the earth to be his own. He rules by law, but the covenant is not merely law. It’s simultaneously a demonstration of grace. God chose Israel by grace because he loved them, not because they were any great shakes. And because all people are sinners, including God’s promised people, his chosen people are sinners, it’s only by grace that there is any covenant blessing at all. The Creator/creature distinction is a covenant relation. It is a Lord/servant relation. We never interact with God as equals. God is Lord of all. And in all of his relations with the world, he speaks and acts as Lord.

This God that we encounter in the Bible, if we’re to rightly understand the Bible, we must understand him to be simultaneously transcendent and imminent. If God is the covenant head, he is highly exalted above his people. That is, he is transcendent, the one who is wholly other, W-H-O-L-L-Y, wholly other. But God is not merely the covenant head, He’s the covenant head. He’s deeply involved with us. He gets in the weeds of our life. He is near – that’s what imminent means. So, God is simultaneously God who is high and lifted up, who also stoops down, as the Scriptures say, to meet us where we are at. This combination of transcendence and imminence results in both authority and present. God is imminent. He is present, but even while present, he’s simultaneously transcendent. By virtue of who he is, he has the right to be obeyed. God, if you will, embodies both might and right. He is authoritative. The Scriptures marvel at the transcendence and imminence of God.

God is knowable. Again, imagine reading the Scriptures if you’re convinced that we cannot possibly know God, it would be an exercise in futility. It would be like a religious studies thing. Like you know, like for me reading, you know, the Bhagavad Gita or something like that, where I just want to know what bizarre things other people believe. But the Scriptures are not a collection of bizarre things that some people believe. The Scriptures are divine speech acts where God engages us. And so, we need to understand that God is knowable. And that doesn’t mean that my thoughts can be God’s thoughts precisely because God is the Creator and I’m not. So, there’s definitely going to be discontinuity between God’s thoughts and my thoughts as I attempt to know God.

God also speaks. Imagine again. Imagine reading the Bible, trying to interpret it correctly. If you didn’t believe God was capable of communication. What would be the point? We see that God’s speech is prevalent and powerful in Genesis 1. He creates the cosmos through his speech, but his speech is also personal. We read earlier Deuteronomy 29:29, “The secret things belong to God, but he’s revealed things to us for our good.” God intentionally speaks. We see from Genesis 11 with the Tower of Babel that God is Lord over language. And we speak. We speak as well, but I would argue that we speak because God speaks, not vice versa. God doesn’t accommodate to us by speaking. He may accommodate his speech to us, but him as a speaker predates us as speakers. So, there’s lots of necessary presuppositions about God when it comes to openly interpreting the Bible.

#### B. The Scriptures are divine speech acts

If we’re going to do justice to the nature of the Bible itself, we have to understand it to be what it is: a collection of human speech acts, certainly, but also a unified divine speech act as well.

#### C. Knowledge of God produces & requires obedience

And then there’s the strange things that happen in the Scriptures, especially as you read through the New Testament. What we find out is that knowledge of God actually produces obedience. Peter wrote 2 Peter 1:3, he says, “His,” (God’s) “divine power has granted to us all things that pertain to life and godliness, through the knowledge of him who called us to his own glory and excellence.” Then verse 5, “For this reason make every effort to supplement your faith with virtue, and virtue with knowledge, and knowledge with self-control.” As we grow in our knowledge of God, we are moved towards obedience because God is God. But it works the other way around too. Obedience of God leads to knowledge. And both are necessary. We obey in order to know. We know in order to obey. Both of those things are true.

#### D. We must understand ourselves as well

But there’s presuppositions about us as well, and we need to have the right understanding of who we are. And again, all of these presuppositions necessary to interpret the Bible correctly are in the Scriptures themselves. What does the Bible say about who we are? Well, prior to belief, we’re not neutral. The Bible, Romans 5, calls us “enemies of God.” We also know that all unbelievers know enough truths about God to be without excuse. We looked at that in Romans 1 with general revelation earlier. That their knowledge of God, it’s more than propositional. They actively suppress and rebel against it. Romans 1 teaches that as well. And so, I would argue, not sure that you should lead in evangelism with this, but I would argue that that response of suppressing and rebelling, it’s first and foremost a stupid response to shake your fist at God and say no to the creator God. That response is also a form of lying, if you will. When we disobey God, we testify to others and to ourselves that God’s word is untrue. We don’t believe it. It’s false. Lying and fighting the truth involve affirmations of falsehood, and not every sentence that an unbeliever utters is false. But disobedience always involves acceptance of, like, a functional atheism. These falsehoods may conflict with true beliefs that sinners hold. And at some level, every unbeliever holds conflicting beliefs. For example, every unbeliever knows that God is Lord. But every very unbeliever states that God is not Lord. That’s a contradiction. Therefore, because of this active cognitive dissonance in both believers and unbelievers, we can’t assume neutrality. We have to instead read the Bible on its terms, following its leads with its presuppositions. Those are some of the theological presuppositions that I think are necessary to read and understand the Bible rightly. We don’t come to those automatically, but they’re necessary. As we read the Scriptures, our understanding of God and of ourselves, it clarifies, it gets more and more accurate. And then as we understand ourselves and God better, our understanding of the Scriptures will grow. And this is what I referred to, again, as that hermeneutical spiral. So how we understand the Scriptures, well, that’s going to be the task of the rest of this class.

<h2 id="lesson-7-hermeneutical-issues-in-translation">Lesson 7: Hermeneutical Issues in Translation</h2>

### I. Problems of Translation

Before we jump into the weeds of how to interpret the Bible, I do want to talk about the Bible that you have in front of you, whatever translation that is. We mentioned earlier in the steps of going from the mind of God to the mind of Todd – inspiration, transmission, and then translation. And we have, especially in English, so many different translations that are at our disposal, so many to choose from. What do we actually have?

And sometimes I think it’s helpful when we’re thinking about hermeneutics to talk about the hermeneutical issues that are involved in translation, and translation is necessarily going to involve some interpretation. And the reason for this is that language is a part of culture, and no two cultures are exactly the same. Cultures will differ. Language is a product of culture, and culture is simultaneously or symbiotically, a product of language. They work mutually on each other.

And, for those of you who’ve taken foreign language before, you know, there’s no such thing as an exact equivalent between the thoughts and words in one language and the thoughts and words in another. You might know that all words have a semantic range, and the semantic range of one word in one language is not going to match up well with the semantic range of a word in a different language.

A good example of this would be in Matthew 1:18-19. This is about the birth of Jesus Christ. And we’re told this, “The birth of Jesus Christ took place in this way. When his mother Mary had been betrothed to Joseph, before they came together she was found to be with child from the Holy Spirit. And her husband Joseph, being a just man and unwilling to put her to shame, resolved to divorce her quickly.” Well, this creates a conundrum for Bible translators, because in our culture it’s very different than what took place in the Ancient Near East culture that Mary and Joseph were a part of. Pledged to be married, husband, wife, divorce. There’s really no equivalent in our culture to this first century Hebrew phenomenon. We wouldn’t talk about needing a divorce in order to break an engagement. What often happens is like the woman hopefully will give the ring back to the guy and then there will be some embarrassing cards and letters having to be sent out and maybe send back some gifts. But it’s not a legal thing. You don’t go to a courthouse to break an engagement. Mary and Joseph weren’t married yet, but to end the engagement required a legal proceeding, and Mary would have been guilty of adultery otherwise. So that’s a bit of a conundrum. The English translation that I read to you just said they resolved to divorce her quietly before they came together. Mary had been betrothed to Joseph, and that’s how they chose to translate those words. But that takes some interpretation. Did they make the right choice?

Another example, John 1:29, “The next day he” (John the Baptist), “saw Jesus coming toward him, and said, ‘Behold the Lamb of God who takes away the sins [recte sin] of the world!’” Well, what if you’re translating the Bible in a part of the world that doesn’t have sheep? What if you’re like in the Arctic or what if you’re in Papua New Guinea? What do you do? What if pigs are the main staple of diet where you’re at and also the main staple of clothing, clothing comes from pigs more than anything else. Would you translate it, “Behold the pig of God that takes away the sins of the world?” “Behold the piglet of God who takes away the sins of the world?” Before you say yes, remember there’s an entire theology of clean and unclean animals in the Bible.

Another example, Isaiah 1:18, “Come now, let us reason together, says the Lord: though your sins are like scarlet, they shall be white as snow; though are red like crimson, they shall be like wool.” Again, what if you’re in a part of the world that there’s no concept of snow? It’s just inconceivable if you’re in a tropical place, Papua New Guinea. And furthermore, they might not even know what wool is. What are you going to do? What if you’re in Japan in the past and the color of purity is red, not white? What are you supposed to do then? Do you translate it the opposite? Then everything gets fouled up. It’s difficult as you go from culture to culture.

### II. Philosophy of Translation

Well, Bible translators have a lot on their plate. And there’s a continuum, if you will, of options that they have. And that’s usually spoken of in terms of formal equivalent on one end and dynamic equivalent on the other end of the continuum.

A formal equivalent translation is going to try to adhere as closely as possible to the original wording of the text. And you might think, well, that’s going to be impossible. And quite frankly, it is. There is no straightforward word-for-word translation because Greek and Hebrew syntax or grammar is very different than English, for example. Nevertheless, in a formal equivalent translation, they’re going to try to do word-for-word translation, maintaining word order where they can. The NASB, New American Standard Bible, would be probably the best example of a formal equivalent translation. Now, if we had a continuum here, it’s not purely formal equivalent. A purely formal equivalent translation would be gibberish because of the grammar and syntax differences as you go from Greek or Hebrew to English. Nevertheless, in the NASB, you’re going to see more word-for-word where they can, and one result of that is, one, the translation is going to be shorter, typically. You’re also going to see a lot of commas and long sentences in the NASB. It’s almost like reading the Puritans who never saw a comma or semicolon that they didn’t like.

On the other end of the continuum are dynamic equivalent translations, and the goal of a dynamic equivalent translation is to reproduce thought-for-thought rather than word-for-word. And like the NIV or the CSB, these are a good example of a pretty dynamic equivalent translation. Sometimes in some instances the idioms or figures of speech are interpreted and replaced. An example of that in the NIV where language of sexual intimacy is used and a Hebrew idiom would be, “And Adam knew his wife,” for example. Well, they’ll just translate that for you or they’ll interpret it for you, “And Adam had sex with his wife,” they might say. There they are taking a Hebrew idiom and they’re interpreting it for you in case “knowing someone” is not the culturally relevant way of talking about sexual intimacy.

Off the continuum, in my estimation, something to the other side of dynamic equivalence would be a paraphrase and here explanatory words and phrases that aren’t found in the original, but the paraphraser feels are necessary to give the sense of the passage, those are included in that. And I would argue, like Eugene Peterson’s, The Message, is a paraphrase. It’s not really a translation. I know in the preface to The Message it says that it’s a translation. I would argue it’s probably more of a paraphrase.

Now in all of this, even with paraphrases, I’m not a translation basher. I’m not. I delight in all the different Bible translations that we have. There’s some that I prefer over others, but I think all of them have strengths. So, a couple of things that I want you to remember about this. First off, accuracy is not related to where you’re at on the continuum. That is, I know that with formal equivalent translations, they will often advertise as the most accurate Bible translation precisely because it is a formal equivalent translation. I just want you to know that’s false. I don’t think that’s the case at all. Every Bible translator is endeavoring to produce the most accurate translation that they can. No one is saying, “Well, ours is super readable, but not that accurate, but it’s super readable, so buy it anyway.” No one wants that and no one is advertising as being, “We’re the fourth most accurate Bible translation out there, so it’s worth the small amount of money we want you to pay.” No one is doing that. Every Bible translator picks where they’re at on the continuum because they think that’s the best place to be accuracy wise. It’s a difference in philosophy. It’s not a difference in desire for accuracy.

And second, you need to know that wherever a Bible translator chooses to be on that continuum of formal equivalent on one end, dynamic equivalent on the other end, they’re not always going to be consistent in where they’re at. I have a whole book that was written showing how in some places the NIV is more formal than the NASB and vice versa. So, they’re never going to be exactly true to their philosophy compared to other translations. But that’s okay. Bible translation is very, very difficult. So, delight in the riches that we have. Also, I guess one more thing, this would be the third thing, go to the front of your Bible and read what the Bible translators say their philosophy of translation is. Again, I’m not a translation basher, but I do want you to know what you have in front of you and use that knowledge to your advantage.

### III. Qualities of a Good Translation

Now, what would be qualities of a good translation, in my estimation? Well, first off, I think it should be based on the best Greek and Hebrew manuscripts. Now I say best, and that’s going to raise a big question that is debated. Why is that? Because the King James Version is based on a manuscript family, or tradition, called the Textus Receptus or the Received Text. In 1516, the great Catholic humanist scholar Erasmus used four Greek manuscripts from the 12th century. He didn’t have the last six verses of the Book of Revelation. He had to take those six verses from the Vulgate, the Latin translation, and he put together a critical edition, if you will. Now, since that was very, very important for the Reformation, Martin Luther, for example, will say if Erasmus hadn’t done what he did, there would have been no Protestant Reformation. So having a good Greek text was of vital importance. Since then, since Erasmus, we have found at least 5,000 additional Greek manuscripts, some from at least a thousand years earlier than that.

So, the question is, there’s largely two families of Greek manuscripts. One is the Textus Receptus, the other is referred to as just the Critical Text, which is a group of scholars have performed this critical analysis to decide amongst all the different manuscripts they have what is the best, most reliable Greek that we have. If you were to open up a Greek text in most published form, you would find a page where a lot of it would be Greek text, and down below is what we call the Apparatus, and that lists all the different variants. And it’s the job of these critical scholars to decide what belongs up above in the Greek text and what goes down below in “this is a variant.” The KJV is based on this Textus Receptus. NASB, ESV, NIV, CSB, fill in the blank here with whatever your favorite translation, probably coming from what I described as this Critical Text. There are not enormous differences between the two, but there are some and there’s at least one very significant difference in 1 John 5:6-8. I’m going to read the ESV version and then I’ll read the KJV to you. The ESV reads this way 1 John 5:6-8, “This is he who came by water and blood – Jesus Christ; not by the water only but by the water and the blood. And the Spirit is the one who testifies, because the Spirit is [recte the] truth. For there are three that testify: the Spirit and the water and the blood; and these three agree.” If you were to open up a KJV, you would read this…same passage, “This is he that came by water and blood, even Jesus Christ; not by water only, but by water and blood. And it is the Spirit that beareth witness, because the Spirit is truth. For—” And then here’s the difference, “For there are three that bear record in heaven, the Father, the Word, and the Holy Ghost: and these three are one. And there are three that bear witness in earth, the Spirit, and the water, and the blood: and these three agree in one.”

You might notice there’s a really strong Trinitarian verse that’s in the KJV that’s not in the ESV, and I wish that that verse were in the Bible because if it were there, it would be the clearest Trinitarian verse in the entire Bible. The problem is, I don’t think it actually belongs. There are only four extant Greek manuscripts that have the inclusion of Father, Son, and Holy Spirit in verse seven. Three of those were written in the margins even; one of them comes from the 16th century. It’s also interesting to me that no one who argued for the Trinity in the second, third and fourth centuries ever cited 1 John 5:7, a book of the Bible that had strong acceptance at this time. No one went to 1 John 5:7 to support the doctrine of the Trinity. Why not? It seems pretty obvious. That would have been a good one to pull out in the Council of Nicaea when people were arguing with the Arians about the equality of the Father and the Son. But they didn’t. Why? Probably because it wasn’t actually there. That would be my guess. Also, John really doesn’t reference the Holy Spirit, although he has dozens of references to the Spirit. It seems to me a glaring failure. This is me bashing one translation, and I just told you earlier, I don’t really bash translations, but I do think a glaring failure of the NKJV is that the editors kept the Trinitarian reference in 1 John 5:7 when I think they knew it doesn’t actually belong there. It’s not really even in most of the Received Text manuscripts that we have.

So, the question is, when it comes down to what’s the most accurate, right, the most accurate Greek manuscripts? Do you want the one where we have the most copies or do you want the one with the oldest copies? If you want most copies, then you would go with the Received Text. If you think oldest is more reliable, then you would go with that Critical Edition. And that would be the translations of basically everything that’s not King James.

A good translation should be based on the latest knowledge of language and culture it seems to me. The Dead Sea Scrolls here were an enormous find. We gained invaluable insight into the cultural and religious life of the period during which many of the biblical manuscripts were written. That’s very, very helpful when it comes to translation theory. They’re also invaluable for establishing the semantic domain of biblical words. And we always want to be updating our translation based on good scholarship of what culture and language was like at the time that the Scriptures were written.

Obviously, it goes without saying, hopefully, that a good translation should be accurate, but I’ll say it anyway. There are some versions of, say, John 21:2, where the Greek text literally says, ”And the ones of Zebedee,” no doubt referring to the sons of Zebedee, who are James and John. But there’s some translators who just translate it instead of “And the Sons of Zebedee,” they just say, “My brother James and I.” Well, but it doesn’t say “My brother James and I,” it says, “And the ones of Zebedee.” That’s a bit too interpretive it seems to me.

Should be understandable. The Scripture doesn’t work like magic. We have to understand it. Our traditions, our translations, I’m sorry, must reflect the way language is used right now. And language does change over time. Look, if you will, at the NASB rendering of 2 Corinthians 10:13, “But we will not boast beyond our measure, but within the measure of the sphere which God apportioned to us as a measure, to reach even as far as you.” I think that’s almost meaningless. It’s just really difficult to understand. Does it reflect Greek word order? Yeah, largely. It’s pretty minimal as well. And so, it has value. But if you’re just, if you’re reading devotionally, that’s a tough one. Now, understandable may not mean accurate and accurate may not always mean understandable. So how do you strike the balance there?

Our translation should be contemporary. So, there should be no end to the making or updating of translations. Rapid changes in English require constant revision to our translations and words change. Make sure what they mean is what they mean today. That is, make sure the words you use in Bible translation are what the words actually mean today. You can think of obvious words that have changed meaning over time. I think in my own lifetime, the word ‘gay’ has—it has dramatically changed. It’s been said, I think this is true, if Shakespeare came back today, it’s estimated that he would recognize less than five out of every nine words spoken in English. That’s amazing, because who has had more influence over English than Shakespeare and probably the KJV of the Bible. Those are the standards of English for hundreds of years.

Should be universal and there’s probably a place for narrow dialect translations. But generally speaking, I don’t think that Bible translation should be limited to a narrow dialect at all. This is just me, but I think Bible translation should be dignified. We shouldn’t blush as we read them. Now, the Bible is an earthy book and it doesn’t pull punches, but the Bible does or was written in a way that we can obey what we’re told to do, which is read the Bible to our kids. So shouldn’t use crude language for shock effect. I’ll just leave it at that.

Should avoid theological bias. This is an accuracy thing as well. Roman Catholic theology would suggest and teach the perpetual virginity of Mary. But Matthew 1:25 is problematic there where it reads that Joseph “knew her not until she had given birth to a son.” The clear implication being that after she gave birth to a son that Joseph and Mary consummated their marriage. Well, that’s inconvenient for the Roman Catholics. So, in the Jerusalem Bible, which is a Catholic translation, they just translate it. “Even though Joseph knew not Mary.” Period. And they leave out the “Until she had given birth to a son.” That feels like you’re cooking the books just a bit. Let’s avoid theological bias. Let’s have our theology be influenced by our Bible translations, not the other way around.

So those are just some thoughts on Bible translation. If I were to tell you the story of Bible translations in my own life, it would run from KJV to…I remember getting my first NIV as a seventh grader and reading that, just being awed at how wonderful that translation was. I used the NIV up until I got to college. Joined the Navigators was told that, you know, we’re serious studiers of the Bible and the NASB is more accurate and better for Bible study. I believed it because that’s what I was told. So, I used the NASB for years after that. I went to Western Seminary where I took a class from a fellow named Earl Radmacher, who had been the chief editor on something called the Nelson Study Bible, which used the NKJ translation of the Bible. I didn’t know any better, it was a required text, and I just assumed that was the standard at Western Seminary. So, I bought it and used it. I never—it wasn’t until I had already bought copies of it that I realized that I can use whatever Bible translation I want and I’ll be taught Greek and Hebrew anyway there. But I used the NKJ for years. And then I went to seminary and did my doctoral work, and a lot of my professors were involved in the ESV translation. And so, then I started using that, and I really like the ESV. But now at our church right now, our pew Bible is the CSB. We made that decision because we couldn’t buy the NIV 1984 version anymore to replace the NIVs that we had. We decided to go with the CSB instead because we thought it was most like the NIV, and we preferred that over the NIV 2011. And it’s our core conviction that we preach out of whatever the pew Bible is. And so now I have a CSB, and that’s what I’ve been using a lot in this class, although there’s a lot of ESV sprinkled in there as well. If you were to ask me to quote like John 3:16 to you, I couldn’t do it in any one translation. It would be a hodgepodge of King James, NIV, NASB, and ESV. All that to say I’m not a translation basher. I probably came to Christ reading the NIV. I use the ESV for most of my scholarship, and now I use the CSB almost exclusively because of who I publish with (Broadman and Holman) and what our church uses. But delight in the many English translations. You have just to be aware of what you have in front of you.

<h2 id="lesson-8-principles-of-hermeneutics">Lesson 8: Principles of Hermeneutics</h2>

### I. Axiom #5: Hermeneutics is Intuitive & not Complicated

We are finally delving into the practical application of hermeneutics, which serves as the foundation for how we interpret the Bible. I want to begin with Axiom #5: Hermeneutics is intuitive and not complicated. We are not Gnostics, and I am not here to provide secret keys or hidden knowledge to unlock the Bible. In reality, hermeneutics is largely intuitive; you have learned how to interpret communication throughout your life. You navigate different literary genres and complex social interactions every day seamlessly. My goal is not to give you "secret sauce," but to make you aware of what you already know so that you can be intentional with it.

### II. Axiom #6: Faithful Bible Interpreters Must Be Fervent Bible Readers

To be a good Bible interpreter, you must be a faithful Bible reader. Unfortunately, biblical illiteracy is a significant issue in America, even among those who claim to be born again. Statistics from the Barna Research Group highlight this tragedy: fewer than half of American adults can name the four Gospels, many Christians cannot identify more than two or three disciples, and 60% of Americans cannot name five of the Ten Commandments. Perhaps most concerning is that 81% of born-again Christians believe the phrase "God helps those who help themselves" is a Bible verse, which is actually antithetical to the gospel. Other polls show people mistakenly believe Joan of Arc was Noah’s wife or that Billy Graham preached the Sermon on the Mount.

The problem is not just familiarity; we must understand the Bible as a whole to interpret it correctly. Many people avoid the "hard" parts of the Bible, but the truly difficult passages are not the confusing ones—they are the ones that challenge our pride and sensibilities. To develop an overall understanding of the Bible’s message, you must read it consistently. I recommend a goal of reading through the entire Bible each year. It is a critical practice for everyone, especially pastors. Therefore, Axiom #6 states: Faithful Bible interpreters must be fervent Bible readers. You will grow more comfortable with the Bible as you read it more and more.

### III. Axiom #7: The Three Most Important Things in Hermeneutics are Context, Context, & Context

The most important principle in biblical hermeneutics is Axiom #7: The three most important things in hermeneutics are context, context, and context. Just as location is the key to real estate, context is the chief determiner of meaning. Everyone understands this intuitively; no one likes being taken out of context because it manipulates their words to mean something other than what they intended. When public figures are caught saying something foolish, their first excuse is often that they were "taken out of context," because they know that context is the standard for accountability. If we dislike being taken out of context, we should be careful not to do the same to the Lord’s word.

There are different levels of context. The broadest level is the entire Bible, based on the principle of "letting Scripture interpret Scripture." Because of divine authorship, the Bible is one unified story, and clearer passages should be used to interpret less clear ones. Scholars often categorize context into three "horizons":
1. The **textual horizon**: The immediate context, such as the paragraph or sentence.
2. The **epochal horizon**: Where we are in redemptive history and which covenant is in play.
3. The **canonical horizon**: How the passage fits into the story of Scripture as a whole.

### IV. Interpreting the Meaning of Matthew 18:20 in Context

Matthew 18:20, "For where two or three are gathered together in my name, I am there among them," is perhaps the most taken-out-of-context verse by well-meaning Christians. It is commonly used to validate prayer meetings, but looking at the immediate context (verses 15–20), it is clear this is actually a passage about church discipline. 

When we look at the broader context of chapter 18, we see Jesus teaching on humility, the danger of sin, the Parable of the Lost Sheep, and the necessity of forgiveness. The church discipline passage is sandwiched between teachings on restoration and forgiveness, suggesting that discipline is actually a "rescue mission" for wandering sinners. The process involves private confrontation, then bringing two or three witnesses—the same "two or three" mentioned in verse 20—to help vet the situation. If the person refuses to repent, the church may remove them from fellowship, not to shun them, but to acknowledge that the community no longer has confidence in their profession of faith. 

Jesus promises his authoritative presence in this process because the church is doing the work of heaven—binding and loosing. While it is not wrong to ask for Jesus's presence in a prayer meeting, that is not the specific meaning of this verse. By studying the context, we see that verse 20 is about the Lord’s authoritative presence in the church’s restorative work of discipline.

<h2 id="lesson-9-context-in-redemptive-history">Lesson 9: Context in Redemptive History</h2>

### I. Interpreting a portion of Scripture by identifying its place in redemptive history

It is essential to remember that every portion of Scripture finds its place in the Bible as a whole. We must locate each passage within redemptive history because the Bible is a story with a definite plot line, featuring a setting, characters, and a problem to be fixed. It is not a collection of abstract stories or an anthology of clever religious writings; rather, each part has its place in the canon. The Bible tells the story of creation and fall, the patriarchs, the sojourn in Egypt, the exodus, the constitution of Israel as a nation, the monarchy, the division of the kingdoms, judgment, captivity, and restoration. Throughout this narrative, we are looking for a promised one: the serpent crusher of Genesis 3:15, the heir of Jacob, the prophet like Moses, the Davidic son, and the suffering servant. That person finally arrives in the Messiah, Jesus Christ.

#### A. Axiom 8: there is a progress to revelation & redemptive history; pay attention to that progress

As we move across the storyline of the Bible, the progress of revelation means that some commands given to God’s people earlier might be abrogated or removed by further development in redemptive history. For example, circumcision was the sign of the Abrahamic Covenant and codified in the Mosaic Covenant, but it is not necessary in the New Covenant. Similarly, Jesus abrogated 1400 years of food laws with a single statement about what makes a person clean or unclean. If we ignore this flow and plot line, we read the Bible anachronistically and misinterpret it because we fail to understand the time setting of the passage.

#### B. Read the Bible in flow with what happened before & what will happen after

We need to "go with the flow" of the Scriptures. Most parts of the Bible are designed to be read in sequence, recognizing that what we are reading fits between what happened before and what is to come. While some sections like Psalms or Proverbs have a context where the flow is less critical, for most of the Bible, the movement is vital. We should give the biblical authors credit for having a literary strategy. When we spend years reading a book that was initially designed to be read in one setting, we must be careful not to lose the context or the flow.

#### C. Using 1 Samuel 17 to illustrate how important it is to read in context

To understand the story of David and Goliath, we must start reading much earlier, as the author assumes we have read the previous chapters. 1 Samuel chronicles the transition from an era of judges to an era of prophets and kings, with Samuel serving as the pivot point.

##### 1. Within the context of redemptive history

The people of Israel asked for a king because they wanted to be like other nations, a desire that God eventually granted. Saul was chosen as the king after the people’s own heart—tall and handsome—but he failed to fulfill the primary job description of a king: to fight the enemies of God. After Saul disobeyed, God chose David, a man after His own heart. The anointing of David in 1 Samuel 16:7 highlights that while humans look at outward appearance, the Lord looks at the heart. The Spirit of the Lord came upon David, while it left Saul, setting the stage for the confrontation in chapter 17.

##### 2. Within the context of the book of 1 Samuel

In 1 Samuel 17, the Philistines and Israelites are in a standoff at the Valley of Elah. Goliath, a giant, issues a challenge that terrifies the Israelites. Saul, who should have been the one to fight, hides in his tent. David, the true anointed king, arrives and recognizes that Goliath’s defiance of the armies of Israel is a defiance of God Himself. David refuses Saul’s armor, relying instead on the Lord who rescued him from lions and bears. David’s victory over Goliath demonstrates his kingly worth; he acts as both a warrior king and a shepherd king, teaching the assembly that the battle belongs to the Lord.

### II. Seeing Jesus Christ as the Anointed One/Messiah in the New Testament in the context of 1 Samuel 17

Both Saul and David were "messiahs" in the sense that they were anointed kings of Israel, but neither was *the* Messiah. However, David was so close to the true Messiah that when Jesus arrived 1,000 years later, we recognize Him because He looked and acted like David. Jesus is the one upon whom the Spirit of the Lord rushed without measure. He fought the enemies of God—dying and rising in triumph—and He cares passionately about the glory of God. Furthermore, Jesus is the Good Shepherd who leads and teaches His people. By reading the story in flow and paying attention to the hermeneutical clues provided by the biblical authors, we see that Jesus Christ is the true Son of David, the fulfillment of the entire redemptive narrative.

<h2 id="lesson-10-biblical-theology">Lesson 10: Biblical Theology</h2>

### I. Review of lessons learned in past sessions

#### A. Axiom 2: hermeneutics is first & foremost a theological endeavor
Hermeneutics is first and foremost a theological endeavor. The commitment to finding meaning in the texts carries an a priori affirmation of the reality of authors, including the supreme divine author. Out of the divine character flows authority; God has the right to establish meaning because of who He is and what He has done. We have previously highlighted theological affirmations about the speaker, God, and the reader, acknowledging that God is knowable and communicative.

#### B. Axiom 3: the Bible is God’s speech to us
The Bible is God’s speech to us, where He engages us. Drawing on speech act theory, the primary purpose of speech is to do something. To know what God is trying to do with His speech, we must interpret it correctly so that communication takes place and the speech act accomplishes the author’s desired purpose.

### II. Biblical Theology

#### A. Definition
##### 1. A means of looking at one passage in the Bible & relating it to the entire message of the Bible
Biblical theology is a means of looking at one particular event or passage and relating it to the total picture—the story as a whole. It allows us to relate any Bible story to the whole message of the Bible and, therefore, to ourselves. It encourages a "thick reading" of Scripture, where we read each passage in light of the entire canon rather than as an isolated, "thin" text.

#### B. The task
##### 1. To examine the development of the biblical story from the Old Testament into the New Testament
The task of biblical theology is to examine the development of the biblical story from the Old Testament through to the New Testament, seeking to uncover the interrelationships between the two parts. It is essential for hermeneutics because it makes the difference between the Bible as the Word of God to us now and as merely an interesting historical record.

#### C. Difference between biblical theology & systematic theology
Biblical theology takes the Bible on its own terms, allowing the text to establish the agenda and message without imposing external questions. Conversely, systematic theology involves bringing our own questions, vocabulary, and agendas to the text to answer life’s ultimate questions. You cannot do a biblical theology of something not in the Bible, such as stem cell research, which requires a systematic approach.

### III. Biblical Theology & Hermeneutics

#### A. Scripture is a word act revelation
Scripture is a "word act" revelation. God’s redemptive acts are revelatory of Him and His purposes, but they are never left to speak for themselves; they are always accompanied by verbal communications. There is a general order in Scripture: God announces what He will do, He does it, and then He explains what He did.

#### B. Scripture is a progressive revelation
Our means of interpretation must recognize that Scripture is a progressive revelation. Just like redemption, revelation unfolds in a progressive manner, moving from the beginning of the story toward the climax.

#### C. Axiom 9: we cannot interpret the Bible correctly unless we understand the story of Scripture as a whole
We cannot interpret the Bible correctly unless we understand the story of Scripture as a whole, because every verse fits into that story in a significant way.

##### 1. God’s glory
God’s glory is the main motivation of the biblical story. From creation to the call of Abraham, the exodus, and the life of David, God acts to make much of Himself. The Messiah, Jesus, is the radiance of God’s glory, and all things ultimately point to the glory of God the Father.

##### 2. God’s promise: “I will be your God, and you will be my people. I will dwell in the midst of you”
God redeems His people so that He might dwell with them. This covenant promise is seen in the Garden, the Tabernacle, the Temple, the incarnation of Jesus, the indwelling of the Holy Spirit, and finally in the new heavens and new earth where God will dwell with His perfected people.

##### 3. Jesus as the key figure
Jesus is the key figure in the biblical story. All prophecy, the Davidic line, and the Old Testament promises of salvation are fulfilled in Him. As Jesus taught on the road to Emmaus, all the Scriptures point to Him. Biblical theology schools our hearts and minds to see the world according to the one canon that constitutes the Word of God written.

<h2 id="lesson-11-historical-and-cultural-context">Lesson 11: Historical and Cultural Context</h2>

### I. Introduction & Review

In prior sessions, we discussed the importance of context when interpreting biblical text, focusing primarily on literary, immediate, and canonical context. We emphasized the importance of biblical theology and how a passage fits into the story as a whole. However, there is another essential layer to consider: the cultural or historical context. For example, Proverbs 22:28 states, “Do not move the ancient landmark that your fathers have set.” To apply this proverb today, we must understand what ancient landmarks were and how they were set. We must first ask what the text meant then, before we can determine its significance and application for us now.

### II. Historical Cultural Context

#### A. Considering context to make sense of the passage

One might ask why we need to analyze ancient history or if we need a PhD to understand the Bible. While a PhD is not required, we must consider historical context due to what I call the "scandal of historical particularity."

#### B. Historical particularity

Historical particularity means that God spoke to particular people in a particular language at a particular place in a particular time. He did not speak to me in the 21st century as originally written. The New Testament was written in Koine Greek to people in places like Corinth or Ephesus, not modern cities. As Christians, we recognize that history matters; the Apostle Paul argued in 1 Corinthians 15 that if Christ did not literally rise from the dead in time and space, our faith is futile. Scripture is full of demonstrations that history matters, and we cannot simply ignore the original audience to jump to our own application.

#### C. Axiom #10: For the interpretation of any biblical text to be valid, it must be consistent with the historical cultural context

If an interpretation would have made no sense to the original audience, it is likely incorrect. Historical cultural context is any information about the setting that is necessary or helpful to understand the passage. While we may not always know which details are most helpful, familiarity with the text makes these judgments easier. Because God is an excellent communicator, adequate—though not exhaustive—historical and cultural context can be found within the Bible itself.

### III. Examples

#### A. Revelation 3:14-22

In the letter to the church in Laodicea, Jesus uses local geography to make his point. Laodicea was a banking and medical center, but it lacked its own water source. Nearby Colosse was known for cold water, and Hierapolis for hot water; by the time water reached Laodicea, it was lukewarm. When Jesus says he wants to "vomit" them out because they are lukewarm, he is using their local reality to illustrate their lack of usefulness. While we might interpret "lukewarm" as being "on the fence," the historical context clarifies that Jesus is calling them to be useful, like the hot or cold water, rather than ineffective.

#### B. Matthew 25:1-13

In the parable of the ten virgins, the focus on the groom and the wedding procession is confusing to modern Western readers. However, in the Ancient Near East, this was a standard wedding practice. The groom would travel to the bride’s home, and the wedding party would accompany them back to the groom’s house. The delay of the groom and the need for oil were culturally understood elements. Jesus uses this familiar scenario to teach an eschatological lesson: because he is going away for a long time, his followers must be alert and ready for his return.

### IV. Using Extra-biblical Data

#### A. Axiom #11: The first and best place to look for the historical cultural context is in the Bible itself

I agree with the caution raised by John Sailhamer, who warned against elevating extra-biblical data to the point where the Bible becomes just one of many windows into "holy history." We must privilege the biblical text. However, I disagree with the total exclusion of extra-biblical context, as biblical authors often assume the reader has knowledge of historical events—such as the Feast of Tabernacles or the Feast of Dedication—that are not fully explained in the text.

#### B. A caution against two errors in overemphasizing or undervaluing extra biblical context

We must avoid two extremes: ignoring cultural context entirely, which leads to misapplication, or overemphasizing it, which can make the Bible seem like a textbook on ancient history rather than the Word of God. The goal is to help people understand the text itself, not to impress them with historical trivia.

### V. Example

#### A. 1 Corinthians 11:4-7

Paul’s discussion of head coverings in 1 Corinthians 11 is tied to the cultural meaning of submission in Corinth. If we ignore the context, we might conclude that head coverings are a universal requirement. However, if we understand that Paul is making a general point about proper attitude during worship, we can apply that principle in culturally relevant ways today.

#### B. Personal account

I once witnessed a youth staff use a worship service to express defiance by playing music at a painful volume. Applying the principle from 1 Corinthians 11—that the worship service is not the place to demonstrate rebellion—is a more faithful application than simply mandating head coverings. By understanding the historical situation, we can bridge the gap to our own contemporary context.

<h2 id="lesson-12-cultural-context">Lesson 12: Cultural Context</h2>

### I. Cultural Context

#### A. Definition
Webster defines culture as the concepts, habits, skills, arts, instruments, institutions, etc., of a given people at a given time. We need to recognize that everybody, including ourselves, is a product at some level of his or her culture. Before we can interpret the Bible with any degree of accuracy, we need to recognize this.

#### B. Cultural conditioning
There are three parties especially that we need to consider. First, the biblical author was a product of his times. The cultural conditioning of the author is represented in the biblical text through different writing styles and vocabularies. Second, the original audience was culturally conditioned as well; they had concepts, habits, and institutions that were manifest in the message written to them. Third, the reader—us—brings our own cultural conditioning to the text every time we read it. We read through a cultural lens, and we must be cognizant of this. We have a tendency to make our personal preferences absolutes, believing our cultural sensibilities are the way Christians are supposed to do things. We cannot assume our ambient cultural ethos is best, and we should be suspicious of any claim that our culture has finally arrived.

### II. Accommodation

#### A. First sense of accommodation
God is the initiator of all revelation, and he progressively unfolds it. To do this, God accommodates his revelation to people where they are at that particular time. One sense of accommodation is that God "dumbs things down," but I resist that. The Bible was written in a human social environment, and its analogies are drawn from that environment. It means that in his desire to communicate, God chose one way and not another to speak. Jesus used parables, drawing on the culture of the day, because he wanted to communicate to them. He used images that were relevant and made sense to that specific audience.

#### B. Second sense of accommodation
There is a Creator/creature distinction; God is God, and we are not. We cannot process the divine mind, so God must accommodate his speech to us. This includes figures of speech like anthropomorphism, where human forms are attributed to God (e.g., God having an ear or a hand) to make a point, even though he is Spirit. Another form is zoomorphism, where animal forms are attributed to God (e.g., wings) to communicate care and refuge. A third form is anthropopathism, where human emotions are attributed to God. While some argue God does not experience emotions because they constitute change, I suggest emotions are "imago dei." God is unchanging in his essence, but he is able to respond to us in a manner consistent with his character.

### III. Guidelines for Dealing with Historical and Cultural Context

#### A. Every statement of the Bible is both absolute and relative
Every statement of Scripture is both absolute and relative. It is absolute in that it belongs to the Word of God and is a product of divine omniscience. It is relative in that it is culturally linked; even the language in which it was written is a cultural construct. We must seek the balance of Scripture rather than succumb to historical or theological disjunctions.

#### B. Biblical rationale
Examine carefully the biblical rationale for any saying or command to see at what level it is tied to the biblical corpus of truth. If you cannot develop a biblical theology of a specific practice—such as sackcloth and ashes, a holy kiss, or head coverings—it is likely culturally relative. However, we must identify the broader principle, such as hospitality or humble service, and find a culturally relevant way to practice it.

#### C. Determine how metaphors and models function in the Bible
We hang on to the literalness of elements like the Lord’s Supper because we can develop a biblical theology of wine and bread. Conversely, foot washing was a culturally relevant example Jesus used to communicate humble service. We must distinguish between the cultural example and the underlying biblical theology.

<h2 id="lesson-13-word-meanings-and-word-studies">Lesson 13: Word Meanings and Word Studies</h2>

### I. Words as the Smallest Unit of Meaning

#### A. Words can have multiple meanings (semantic range)
The next section here, we’re going to drill down to the smallest particle of meaning, and that would be the word. I do believe that the sentence is the smallest unit of meeting, but the building blocks of the sentence, of course, are words. How do we use words? How do we use word studies? How do we determine the meaning of a word? Well, in Axiom #12, the most important thing I want you to realize about words is that words have meanings—plural, not meaning. Now, this range of meanings that a word can have is called the semantic range. So, for example, think of the word “trunk.” What could I be referring to when I say “trunk”? Well, there’s a semantic range isn’t there? It could be a tree trunk. It could be a bodily trunk. It could be a trunk in the attic that you put old clothes in. It could be the trunk of a car. It could be the nose of an elephant. There’s a variety of things that it could be. Think of the word “green.” Green has an enormous semantic range. Green is just a color. Green can refer to money. Green can refer to marijuana. Green can refer to being, I think I said, environmentally friendly. A putting surface. Green can refer to being like a newbie or inexperienced in something. Green can mean that you’re sick. Green can mean that you’re jealous. There’s a host of different meanings for these.

#### B. Words have meaning within context
The sentence will give the context that allows us to interpret the words. Any time we hear a word in a specific context, we sort through the possible meanings and apply, if we’re thinking rightly, the correct meaning depending on what the context is. Now imagine, imagine if you were talking to someone and you said to them, “Yeah, I hit the ball up on the green and then was able to putt from there.” And they took your meaning to think you hit the ball up on to a bunch of marijuana? “What are you doing with all that marijuana?” Or, you know, something like that. And you go, “No, no, no, that’s not what I meant.” And they said, “Well, it could mean that, it could mean that and that’s what I chose to make it mean.” And you would talk to this person, you would think, “I am not sure I want to keep talking to you because you’re doing absurd things with my speech. What I meant is what I meant. The sentence meant what I meant it to mean, not whatever you want to make of it just because, you know, you have a dictionary, you can look up different meanings for the words because words have meaning in context.” Now, I just described things, though, that take place all too often in Bible studies where a person will come to the text—and I’ve sat in these studies where people have done this. And at first I was kind of jealous, where they would say something like, “You know, I read this verse and then I looked at this verse up and I discovered there’s a bunch of different possible meanings to that word. And then when I applied the different meanings to that word into this sentence, it’s just like God was just blowing things up for me. It was just awesome.” And my first thought there, I must confess, was like, “Man, they did like real research. I didn’t do that; I just read the verse.” But then, like on second thought, it’s like, I think you did something with the Bible that you would never do with anybody else. That’s ridiculous, what you just did. Words have meanings, not meaning. But a word will have meaning, singular, in context. Now, the exception to this would be where an individual uses a word intentionally with two different meanings. That can be called like “double entendre,” I suppose, but more commonly that would be a pun.

### II. Etymology – the Study of the Original Meaning of a Word

#### A. Understanding what a word means in the original language it was written in
The problem with finding the meaning of words, though, is that we’re not as interested in what a word means in our translation, we want to know what the word meant as it was used in the original language. And so, it seems to me that if the Bible is what we profess it to be, we should make every effort to read it in the original language or use tools wisely to help us with these studies. Now etymology is the study of the original meaning of a word, and it is an important tool in deciphering unknown words and correcting wrong ideas. Something that I’ll say now and I’ll say it at the end, is that the word study, the word study is probably the most valuable Bible study tool that you have at your disposal. And oftentimes you can do a decent word study with the concordance that you have in the back of your Bible. The concordance that you have is probably the most valuable Bible study tool that you have at your disposal.

#### B. Understanding the author’s intention in using the word in context
So, let’s think about how etymology at times can help with this. Here’s a few words. In 1Timothy 3:1, Paul is talking about qualifications for an elder and (some translations might say bishop or overseer), and the Greek word there is episkopos. And episkopos is a compound word where there’s a preposition, epi, which means upon or over, and skopos is sight or seeing. So, you put those two things together and you get “overseer.” And that’s kind of helpful. Kind of helpful. It’s not entirely helpful because when Paul uses the term overseer, or episkopos, he has something very technical in mind, which is more than just someone who looks over. But sometimes, not always, but sometimes we can take the parts of a compound word, put them together, and it gives some insight into what the word means. Apostle is the Greek word apostolos, and it’s basically “to send out.” So, an apostle is a sent out one. Stelo – to send, apo – from. So, a sent out one and that’s what an apostle was, a “sent out one.” But of course, again when apostle is used in the Bible, it’s more than just someone, usually more than just someone who is sent out. There’s a technical meaning with it, especially when Paul is talking about his apostolic authority.

### III. Word Study Difficulties

#### A. A word is more than the sum of its parts
Now there’s exceptions to that. We ought not to think that in every instance the meaning of a word is the sum of its parts. In English we have compound words and sometimes the meaning is bound up in the sum of its parts. So, a rubberband would be a band made from rubber. That’s helpful. But a pineapple is not an apple that’s made from wood. Doesn’t really work that way. Or a butterfly is not an insect made of dairy products. And so, Greek is no exception to that. Sometimes the meaning of the word, yeah, it’s helpful to look at the sum of the parts. Other times, not so much. Not so much. We should be careful to look at a word’s origin. How was it used and what did it mean when it was actually used?

#### B. Word meanings change over time
And we want to keep in mind, and you’re well aware of this in English, that words change in meaning over time. And one example of this that I’ll give later is the word martys or witness and I’ll walk you through the development of what martys has meant in Greek and then how we use it in English. That’s where we get our word “martyr” but it’s usually translated “witness” in our New Testaments.

#### C. Words can have multiple meanings, depending on context
Words have multiple meanings. As I’ve said, there’s a semantic range to them. The 500 most commonly used English words, for example, have an average of 23 meanings. So, the 500 most common English words have a semantic range of 23 possible meanings. That should give us pause when we are careless, I suppose with word meaning.

#### D. A word in English can be used to represent multiple Greek words
There are books and tools at your disposal for synonyms and in the biblical languages there are plenty of synonyms. The English word “receive” Vine’s, which is a lexicon of sorts for laypeople, gives 19 verbs in Greek that translate the English “to receive.” Now, I’m going to give you a caution about Vine’s here in a moment, but at any rate, that it’s a helpful illustration there. For example, there’s the word lambano, which means to “take without feeling.” But proslambano is used in Romans 14:1, it’s an idea of embracing, perhaps without doubting. Dechomai in 1 Corinthians 2:14 is also translated “receive,” and that means to take something eagerly. So, there’s lots of Greek words that can translate into this one English word. We need to be cognizant of that when we’re doing word studies, because if we just go to the back of our Bible, the concordance, and we look up all the instances of an English word, we might not be looking up all the instances of that particular Greek word that was used by the biblical author. So, we need to be careful with that. Another example here, the word “know” in English, there’s a couple of different Greek words that translate into the English word “know.” Oida, for example, is usually used to reflect according to innate knowledge. It’s more cognitive, versus ginosko is more experiential, more experiential. Now those are two different Greek words, but we usually translate it just with one English word “to know.” And another place where this comes into application is the English word “life” translates a number of Greek words. And sometimes we see multiple Greek words that are translated “life” in the same passage. John 10:10-11, for example, “The thief comes only to steal and kill and destroy. I came that they may have life and have it abundantly. I am the good shepherd. The good shepherd lays down his life for the sheep.” Well, what’s going on there? There’s a number of Greek words that translate into the one English word “life,” like zoe, for example. This is oftentimes depending on the context, but oftentimes life in God, it’s qualitative, it’s intensive. This would be, you know, like to have eternal life. But psychē as a word for soul in Greek in the dictionaries/Greek translations that I consulted) or soul is oftentimes translated as “life” as well, and this is the word that we find in John 10:11. So, “The thief comes only to steal and kill and destroy. I came that they may have life…” That is, zoe, this qualitative life, but again, it’s just translated “life” in our English translation. “Have it abundantly. I am the Good Shepherd. The Good Shepherd lays down his,” qualitative life in God? No, he lays down his soul, but we translate it life. The same English words, two very different meanings but there’s two different Greek words there, so it can get a bit confusing at time. That’s why word studies can be helpful. And another word, another biblical word that’s translated “life” is bios. We find that say in 1 John 3:17. All three of those Greek words are commonly translated by one English word, “life.” So, if you’re doing a word study and you just go to the back of your Bible, look up the word “life” in your concordance, you might not be getting a study of one Greek word. You might be getting a study of a variety of Greek words with different meanings and you’re thinking you’re just doing a word study on one word. So be careful. Be careful of that.

#### E. Avoid reverse etymology
Avoid—I got this from D. A. Carson, too—avoid reverse etymology. And this is so true and Christians do this all the time. This is where we take a word that has changed over the years in English and we read that meaning back into the Greek. And here’s what it looks like. Again, you have a Vine’s, you’re not afraid to use it. You’re reading Romans 1:16, “I am not ashamed of the gospel because it is the power of God.” And you look up “power” and you discover that it is the Greek word dunamis, which, when we transliterate it into English, we changed the upsilon (or the u sound) into a y, and it’s d-y-n-a-m-i-s, and that looks like the word “dynamite.” Dynamite. And that’s where we get our word “dynamite” from that Greek word. And so, “Wow, I looked this word up in the Greek, and it’s dunamis, the dynamite of God. It blows things up.” Of course, that’s not what Romans 1:16 is saying at all. The gospel doesn’t blow things up. The gospel is the power of God to reconcile people to God. Or 2 Corinthians 9:7, “God loves a cheerful giver.” Oh, I looked up the word “cheerful,” and it’s the Greek word hilarós. That’s where we get our English word “hilarious.” God wants me laughing all the way to the offering box. Probably not. Probably not. God does not want a hilarious giver; God wants a cheerful, intentional giver. So, avoid reverse etymology. Don’t look a word up in Greek, see how we use it in English and assume that’s what it meant in Greek. Your Bible translators do a better job of taking you from Greek to English than we might, by going from Greek to an English word that has that Greek root. Trust your Bible translators at that point. They usually do pretty good work. By usually I mean they always do really good work.

#### F. Avoid verbal parallelomania – be careful how you use cross references
Avoid verbal parallelomania. This is another one I got from Carson. It’s a kind of mania that seeks parallels to every single thing. If I can find a parallel, that means what I want then you can basically do anything with it. This would be like poor use of cross references where you find a verse in one passage and then you look up in your concordance where that same word is used somewhere else. And then you take the meaning of that other verse and you plug it into this present verse that you’re using. Again, let the Bible speak. Let the biblical authors mean what they say. And the best clue to what a word means in a sentence is the context of the sentence itself. Be careful with cross-references. They’re very, very useful. But again, with great power comes great responsibility. Use them wisely and use them intuitively. Don’t do with the Bible something that you would never do with your friend. Sometimes the appeals to parallels can be useful, though. They provide a broader picture of how a word was often used. Going to a parallel passage or another verse where that same word is used, it can be insightful at times.

#### G. Be careful about linking language with mentality
Be careful about linking language with mentality. Oftentimes I hear things like, “The Hebrew mind was just incapable of distinguishing between first and second causes or between primary and secondary causes.” So, for example, who was responsible for the suffering that that Job underwent? Was it God? Or was it Satan? And I’ve heard people say, “Well, the Hebrew mind was just incapable of distinguishing between primary and secondary causes.” Well, that’s kind of culturally snobbish. I think they weren’t morons. I think they could distinguish between those things. Now, it’s true that some languages have words for concepts that other languages do not. That’s certainly the case. Language does shape the way we think for sure. But be careful about making assumptions there.

#### H. Recognize that different Bible authors will use words differently
And then recognize that different authors will use words differently. And here’s where finding, you know, using your concordance might get you into trouble. The word righteousness, for example, the Greek word dikaiosynē. For Paul, oftentimes that Greek word meant justification. You have to look at the context because Paul doesn’t always use it that way. In Matthew, most of the time when he uses it, it doesn’t necessarily mean justification. It probably means something like righteousness. And so, recognize that different authors might use words differently, the same words.

#### I. Learn to recognize the diversity of metaphors
Learn to recognize the diversity of metaphors as well. Is there a difference between a literal meaning and a metaphorical meaning? Yes, of course there is. And we will discuss how to interpret figures of speech more later. But Jesus was fond of using metaphors. He did often. He said, “I am the door.” I think that would be ridiculous to take that literally. Jesus is said to be the Lion of Judah. Satan is also described as a lion, a prowling lion. Well, I suspect when Jesus is the Lion of Judah, that’s a little bit different than Satan being a prowling lion. And so, we need to recognize that even the same words used metaphorically can have very different meanings. So again, be very careful about assigning a technical meaning to any one word and just plugging that into your study.

### IV. Summary

#### A. The word study is a very valuable Bible study tool
I’ve given you a lot of warnings about how to use word studies. The reason I’m giving you warnings is, again, I’ll repeat it, the word study is a very valuable, very valuable Bible study tool.

#### B. With great power comes great responsibility
But with great power comes great responsibility. Just as it can be a powerful tool, it can be used in very negative ways. And so be very careful about that.

#### C. Use all the tools sensibly
So, do word studies. Use your concordance, use cross-references. Look at how different biblical authors use the same words. Oftentimes there’s insight, but use them sensibly. And I don’t have technical guidance to give you other than just Jesus’s golden rule: don’t do to the biblical authors what you would not want done to yourself.

<h2 id="lesson-14-implications-of-genre">Lesson 14: Implications of Genre</h2>

### I. Genre

#### A. Definition of genre
Genre is a kind of literature, or a literary genre is a literary kind. It refers to the literary form being used by the author. When we talk about literature, we are talking about an interpretive presentation of experience in an artistic form. There is no single literary genre in the Bible; rather, there are genres, plural. There are forms of biblical literature, not a biblical form.

#### B. Using rules of interpretation for reading/understanding a genre
All literary genres come with their own rules of interpretation. We already intuitively know how to do this in our daily lives, such as differentiating between a baseball box score, an opinion piece, a comic strip, a recipe on a box of Pop-Tarts, or a stop sign. We recognize from experience that there are different kinds of literature in the Bible and that those different kinds require different rules for interpreting them. Just as playing football by the rules of baseball would lead to chaos, we must play by the rules of the specific literary genre we are reading.

#### C. Axiom 13#: A genre is a type of literature with the rules that govern its interpretation
This axiom is important because the Bible is literature and we need to read it as such. If you don’t understand the rules governing a specific genre, such as Hebrew poetry, you will get yourself into trouble.

### II. Reading the Bible as literature

#### A. Biblical authors use specific genre to get their point across
The biblical authors were very good writers who chose their literary genre specifically to get their point across. Their working assumption is that you will appreciate and understand the rules of interpretation for that genre and be able to understand what they mean by what they said. The literary genre they choose is the vehicle they pick to get you from where you are to where they want you to be.

#### B. Different genres determine meaning
Understanding literary genre helps us to interpret the Bible literally, which means understanding what the author meant. This includes both the plain literal, which is denotative and explicit, and the figurative literal, which is connotative and relies on specific intention. For example, when Jesus called himself "the door," he used a figure of speech to communicate a specific truth. Understanding the genre helps us discern when an author intends a figurative meaning. Jeremiah 20:14-18 serves as an example where the poetic genre, characterized by hyperbole, helps us understand that Jeremiah is expressing deep despair and frustration rather than literally cursing a specific individual.

### III. Misidentifying a genre may lead to skewed interpretation
If we misidentify a literary genre, it can result in a skewed interpretation. For instance, confusing imprecatory Psalms with law or epistles can lead to error. Similarly, narratives often describe events without prescribing them; there is a difference between what is described and what is prescribed in Scripture. Furthermore, if we treat every proverb as a literal promise of the Lord without exception, we may face disappointment when life does not align with that expectation.

### IV. Caution regarding mislabeling a genre
Mislabeling a biblical genre can be an underhanded way of denying a text’s truthfulness, such as labeling historical narrative as myth to avoid the lessons taught within it. Additionally, principles for interpreting genres can be misused to excuse oneself from the demands of Scripture. For example, some may dismiss the radical commands of Jesus in the Sermon on the Mount by claiming they are merely proverbial, thereby avoiding the call to live out those teachings. We must be careful not to use genre identification as a way to excuse ourselves from obeying what is written.

<h2 id="lesson-15-interpreting-biblical-narrative-part-1">Lesson 15: Interpreting Biblical Narrative (Part 1)</h2>

### I. Nature of Historical Narrative

#### A. Characteristics
Well, let’s begin looking at the individual biblical genres by considering biblical narrative. And I don’t see any difference between historical narrative and biblical narrative. I’m going to use those terms interchangeably. Regarding historical narrative then, how did the early church know if something was historical in the Bible? There are some made-up stories (the parables) and then there are some true stories, these are what I’ll call the historical narratives. How did the church understand whether something was historical or not? And the answer was basically this: Does it claim to be historical narrative? Does it come with the markers of historical narrative? There are clues for every literary genre, and narrative is no different. So basically, what they did, the early church, they evaluated the literary genre. So, then you have to ask the question: so, is this a historical narrative or is it a made-up story, something like a parable would be? Well, then you would look at the clues that would be there. For example, in the parables, Jesus usually said something along the lines of, “The kingdom of Heaven is like…” So, he’s making a formal comparison between the story that he’s telling and the Kingdom of Heaven. Then you have to ask yourself, once you’ve said, “Well, I see the markers of historical narrative, that is the setting is established, characters are identified, there is a plot, the biblical characters or the characters in this story are treated as though they are true and real people.” Then you have to ask yourself, “What’s the relationship between what the author meant as he’s telling this story, and what really happened?” And you might think that question wouldn’t have to be asked, but it started to be asked in pretty much like the 1700s. Up until the 1700s, the relationship between what the biblical author meant and what actually happened, well it was univocal, they were one and the same.

#### B. Changes because of the Renaissance
And then with the Protestant Reformation and the Renaissance that followed, people began to ask “why” questions and they weren’t willing to accept things on a say-so basis.
